<a id='question-098'></a>

### Question 098: Medical Parasitology: Part 2 Focus 48 (Mechanical:)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the life cycle, pathogenesis, and host immune response to *Trypanosoma cruzi*, specifically the mechanism of parasite invasion into host cells and the resulting cellular damage. (理解 *Trypanosoma cruzi* 的生命周期、发病机制以及宿主免疫反应，特别是寄生虫入侵宿主细胞的机制以及由此造成的细胞损伤。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old male agricultural worker from rural Paraguay presents to a clinic in the United States 10 years after immigrating. He reports a 3-month history of progressive fatigue, intermittent low-grade fever, and worsening palpitations. He denies recent travel outside the US. Physical examination reveals mild cardiomegaly and a new apical holosystolic murmur. An electrocardiogram (ECG) shows nonspecific ST-T wave changes and evidence of left ventricular hypertrophy. An echocardiogram confirms dilated cardiomyopathy with apical akinesis. Serological testing is positive for antibodies against *Trypanosoma cruzi*. A myocardial biopsy reveals interstitial myocarditis with evidence of cardiomyocyte necrosis and parasitic amastigotes within macrophages. The patient is diagnosed with chronic Chagas disease. Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the cardiomyocyte damage observed in this patient?

(A) Direct lysis of cardiomyocytes by parasite-derived proteases.
(B) Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) targeting infected cardiomyocytes.
(C) Release of reactive oxygen species (ROS) and nitric oxide (NO) by activated macrophages attempting to kill intracellular amastigotes.
(D) Complement-mediated lysis of cardiomyocytes expressing parasite surface antigens.
(E) T-helper 1 (Th1) cell-mediated cytotoxicity directly targeting infected cardiomyocytes.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old male, agricultural worker from Paraguay (endemic area for Chagas disease), immigrated 10 years ago. This history strongly suggests potential exposure to *Trypanosoma cruzi*.
- **Symptoms:** Progressive fatigue, low-grade fever, palpitations (suggestive of cardiac involvement).
- **Physical Exam:** Cardiomegaly, apical holosystolic murmur (suggestive of dilated cardiomyopathy).
- **Investigations:** ECG (nonspecific changes, LVH), Echocardiogram (dilated cardiomyopathy, apical akinesis - classic findings in Chagas cardiomyopathy). Serology positive for *T. cruzi* antibodies. Myocardial biopsy shows interstitial myocarditis, cardiomyocyte necrosis, and intracellular amastigotes within macrophages.
- **Diagnosis:** Chronic Chagas disease with cardiomyopathy.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely mechanism of cardiomyocyte damage.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Chagas Disease Pathogenesis:** Caused by *Trypanosoma cruzi*. The parasite has a complex life cycle involving insect vectors (triatomine bugs) and mammalian hosts.
- **Invasion:** Metacyclic trypomastigotes enter the host via the bug's feces, penetrate cells (muscle, macrophages) via receptor-mediated endocytosis (e.g., involving mannose receptors, complement receptors, lectins).
- **Intracellular Cycle:** Inside the host cell, trypomastigotes transform into amastigotes (intracellular replicative form). Amastigotes replicate within a parasitophorous vacuole (PV) inside macrophages and other cells (including cardiomyocytes).
- **Immune Response:** The host mounts both innate and adaptive immune responses. Macrophages phagocytose trypomastigotes and amastigotes. Activated macrophages attempt to kill the intracellular parasites using mechanisms like ROS and NO production. T cells (CD4+ and CD8+) are also involved.
- **Cardiomyocyte Damage:** The chronic phase of Chagas disease is characterized by persistent inflammation and damage to the heart muscle. While direct parasite invasion of cardiomyocytes can occur, a significant portion of the damage is thought to be immune-mediated. Macrophages, recruited to the heart, phagocytose parasites and release inflammatory mediators. The attempt by macrophages to kill intracellular amastigotes via ROS and NO production can also cause collateral damage to the host cardiomyocytes. Cytokines like TNF-alpha and IL-1beta contribute to inflammation and myocardial injury. Autoimmunity may also play a role.
- **Option (C) Rationale:** Activated macrophages are key players in controlling *T. cruzi* infection. They phagocytose parasites and attempt to kill them intracellularly. This process involves the production of microbicidal molecules like ROS (e.g., superoxide, hydrogen peroxide) and NO. While these molecules are effective against the parasite, they can also damage host cell components, including cardiomyocytes, leading to necrosis and dysfunction. This mechanism aligns well with the observed interstitial myocarditis and cardiomyocyte necrosis in the biopsy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Direct lysis of cardiomyocytes by parasite-derived proteases. While *T. cruzi* does possess proteases, the primary mechanism of cardiomyocyte damage in chronic Chagas disease is not considered to be direct lysis by these enzymes. The damage is more strongly linked to the host's inflammatory response and immune-mediated mechanisms.
- (B): Antibody-dependent cell-mediated cytotoxicity (ADCC) targeting infected cardiomyocytes. ADCC involves antibodies binding to target cells (infected cardiomyocytes in this case) and NK cells or other cytotoxic cells recognizing the Fc portion of the antibody, leading to cell lysis. While antibodies are produced against *T. cruzi*, ADCC is not considered the primary mechanism of cardiomyocyte damage in Chagas cardiomyopathy. The main target of ADCC is typically extracellular parasites or infected macrophages.
- (D): Complement-mediated lysis of cardiomyocytes expressing parasite surface antigens. Complement activation can occur in Chagas disease, but complement-mediated lysis of cardiomyocytes is not the main mechanism of damage. Complement can opsonize parasites for phagocytosis or contribute to inflammation, but direct lysis of cardiomyocytes by complement is less likely to be the predominant cause of the observed pathology.
- (E): T-helper 1 (Th1) cell-mediated cytotoxicity directly targeting infected cardiomyocytes. Th1 cells are crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens like *T. cruzi*. They produce IFN-gamma, which activates macrophages. While CD8+ T cells can kill infected cells, direct Th1-mediated cytotoxicity against cardiomyocytes is not the primary mechanism. The damage is more often attributed to macrophage activation and the resulting inflammatory response and collateral damage from ROS/NO production.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature | *Trypanosoma cruzi* (Chagas) | *Plasmodium falciparum* (Malaria) | *Leishmania* spp. (Leishmaniasis) | *Toxoplasma gondii* (Toxoplasmosis) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Primary Host Cell Damage Mechanism in Chronic Disease** | Macrophage-mediated ROS/NO production causing collateral damage to cardiomyocytes | Sequestration of infected RBCs in microvasculature, cytokine storm | Macrophage-mediated ROS/NO production causing tissue damage (skin, viscera) | Macrophage-mediated ROS/NO production causing tissue damage (brain, eye) |
| **Key Immune Mediator** | Activated Macrophages (ROS, NO) | TNF-alpha, Cytokine Storm | Activated Macrophages (ROS, NO) | Activated Macrophages (ROS, NO) |
| **Target Organ (Chronic)** | Heart (cardiomyopathy) | Brain (cerebral malaria), Spleen | Spleen, Liver, Bone Marrow (visceral), Skin (cutaneous) | Brain (encephalitis), Eye (chorioretinitis) |
| **Intracellular Form** | Amastigote | Merozoite (in RBCs), Sporozoite (in liver) | Amastigote | Bradyzoite |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Chagas cardiomyopathy results from chronic inflammation and immune-mediated damage to the heart muscle caused by *Trypanosoma cruzi*. A key mechanism involves the collateral damage inflicted by activated macrophages attempting to kill intracellular amastigotes via the production of reactive oxygen species (ROS) and nitric oxide (NO). Differentiate this from other parasitic diseases where macrophage activation also causes damage (e.g., Leishmaniasis, Toxoplasmosis), but the specific target organ (heart) is characteristic of Chagas disease. Also, distinguish from direct parasite lysis or antibody/complement-mediated mechanisms, which are less prominent in the pathogenesis of Chagas cardiomyopathy.

<a id='question-099'></a>

### Question 099: Medical Parasitology: Part 2 Focus 49 (transport)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the intracellular transport mechanisms utilized by *Toxoplasma gondii* to disseminate throughout the host, specifically its ability to exit the host cell via a specialized secretion system and infect other cell types, including neurons. (理解弓形虫 (*Toxoplasma gondii*) 在宿主体内利用的细胞内运输机制，特别是其通过专门的分泌系统离开宿主细胞并感染其他细胞类型（包括神经元）的能力。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old pregnant woman presents to her primary care physician complaining of flu-like symptoms (fatigue, myalgia, low-grade fever) that began approximately 2 weeks ago. She reports recently adopting a stray cat, which she allowed to roam freely in her home. She denies any recent travel or exposure to contaminated food or water. Her physical examination is unremarkable except for mild cervical lymphadenopathy. Serological testing reveals positive IgG and IgM antibodies against *Toxoplasma gondii*. An MRI of her brain shows multiple ring-enhancing lesions, predominantly in the basal ganglia and thalamus. Given the patient's pregnancy and neurological findings, the physician is concerned about congenital transmission and potential long-term neurological sequelae. Which of the following mechanisms is primarily responsible for the dissemination of *Toxoplasma gondii* from infected cells to adjacent cells, including neurons in the central nervous system?

(A) Phagocytosis of infected cells by macrophages, followed by parasite release within the macrophage lysosome.
(B) Exocytosis of merozoites via the host cell's secretory pathway.
(C) Active secretion of tachyzoites into the extracellular space via a specialized apical organelle, followed by invasion of adjacent cells.
(D) Fusion of the parasitophorous vacuole containing bradyzoites with the host cell membrane, releasing bradyzoites extracellularly.
(E) Transfer of infected cells via the bloodstream to distant organs, followed by parasite egress through cell-cell junctions.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** Pregnant woman, 35 years old. Pregnancy increases susceptibility to severe toxoplasmosis and risk of congenital transmission.
- **Symptoms:** Flu-like symptoms (fatigue, myalgia, fever) + cervical lymphadenopathy. Classic presentation of acute toxoplasmosis.
- **Exposure History:** Adopted stray cat allowed to roam freely. Cats are definitive hosts for *Toxoplasma gondii*, shedding oocysts in feces. Exposure to cat feces (e.g., cleaning litter box) is a major risk factor.
- **Serology:** Positive IgG and IgM against *T. gondii*. Indicates acute infection (IgM) and past/present infection (IgG).
- **Imaging:** MRI shows multiple ring-enhancing lesions in basal ganglia/thalamus. Characteristic finding of cerebral toxoplasmosis, especially in immunocompromised individuals, but can occur in immunocompetent pregnant women.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of *T. gondii* dissemination from infected cells to adjacent cells, including neurons. This focuses on the parasite's motility and invasion strategy.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- *Toxoplasma gondii* is an obligate intracellular parasite.
- **Invasion:** Tachyzoites invade host cells (nucleated cells) via apical organelles (micronemes, rhoptries, dense granules). Micronemes secrete proteins that facilitate attachment and gliding motility. Rhoptries secrete proteins that form a parasitophorous vacuole (PV) membrane around the parasite within the host cell cytoplasm.
- **Intracellular Replication:** Tachyzoites replicate rapidly within the PV.
- **Dissemination:** *T. gondii* disseminates throughout the body by infecting new cells. This occurs primarily through two mechanisms:
    1.  **Cell-to-cell spread:** Tachyzoites actively move within the host cell cytoplasm, often propelled by the actin-myosin ring at the posterior end of the parasite. They then position themselves adjacent to the host cell membrane. The apical organelles (specifically the apical complex) are re-oriented towards the host cell membrane. The parasite then secretes proteins via its apical organelles to puncture the host cell membrane and invade the adjacent cell. This process is often described as "extravasation" or "egress" followed by invasion. The specialized apical organelle involved in this process is crucial for motility and invasion.
    2.  **Hematogenous spread:** Infected cells can rupture, releasing tachyzoites into the bloodstream, which can then infect other cells or be phagocytosed by immune cells (like macrophages), potentially leading to dissemination. However, cell-to-cell spread is considered the primary mechanism for CNS invasion.
- **Bradyzoites:** In chronic infection, tachyzoites differentiate into slow-growing bradyzoites, which form tissue cysts (often in the brain and muscle). Bradyzoites are released from ruptured cysts, but their role in active dissemination is less significant than tachyzoites.
- **Apical Organelles:** Micronemes (adhesion, gliding), Rhoptries (PV formation), Dense granules (modification of PV, host cell manipulation). The apical complex is essential for invasion and egress/invasion cycle.

##### 3. Mechanism Specific to the Question Stem:
The question specifically asks about dissemination *from infected cells to adjacent cells*. This points directly to the cell-to-cell spread mechanism involving the apical organelles. The parasite uses its apical complex to actively puncture the host cell membrane and invade the neighboring cell. This process is mediated by the secretion of proteins from the apical organelles.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Phagocytosis of infected cells by macrophages is a host immune response, not the primary mechanism of *T. gondii* dissemination. While macrophages can become infected, the parasite doesn't primarily rely on being released from macrophage lysosomes to spread. The parasite actively invades new cells.
- (B): Exocytosis via the host cell's secretory pathway is not the mechanism used by *T. gondii*. The parasite uses its own specialized apical organelles to actively puncture the host cell membrane and invade adjacent cells.
- (D): Bradyzoites are associated with chronic infection and tissue cysts. They are released when cysts rupture, but this is not the primary mechanism for the active dissemination of tachyzoites responsible for acute infection and CNS spread. Furthermore, bradyzoites are typically released extracellularly from cysts, not via fusion of the PV with the host cell membrane.
- (E): Transfer of infected cells via the bloodstream (hematogenous spread) does occur, but the question specifically asks about spread to *adjacent* cells. While infected cells can travel via blood, the primary mechanism for invading *neighboring* cells, especially neurons in the CNS, is the active cell-to-cell spread mediated by the apical organelles. Egress through cell-cell junctions is not the described mechanism for *T. gondii*.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | *Toxoplasma gondii* (Tachyzoite) | *Trypanosoma cruzi* (Epimastigote/Trypomastigote) | *Plasmodium falciparum* (Merozoite) | *Leishmania* (Amastigote) |
| :------------------ | :-------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------- | :----------------------- |
| **Primary Host Cell** | Nucleated cells                  | Nucleated cells                                 | Hepatocytes, RBCs                  | Macrophages              |
| **Dissemination**    | Cell-to-cell (apical organelles), hematogenous | Cell-to-cell (trans-sialidase), hematogenous | Hematogenous (rupture RBCs)        | Hematogenous (macrophage spread) |
| **Intracellular Location** | Parasitophorous Vacuole (PV)     | PV (Epimastigote), Cytoplasm (Trypomastigote)   | Cytoplasm (RBCs)                   | PV                      |
| **Key Virulence Factor for Spread** | Apical organelles (micronemes, rhoptries) | Trans-sialidase                              | None (RBC rupture)                | None (macrophage spread) |
| **Typical Disease**  | Toxoplasmosis (encephalitis)      | Chagas disease (cardiomyopathy, megaesophagus) | Malaria (cyclic fever, hemolysis) | Leishmaniasis (skin, visceral) |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
*Toxoplasma gondii* tachyzoites utilize a specialized apical complex (micronemes, rhoptries) to actively invade adjacent host cells, facilitating its dissemination, particularly within the central nervous system. This mechanism contrasts with parasites that rely solely on hematogenous spread or passive transfer. Key differentials include other intracellular parasites like *Leishmania* (spreads within macrophages) and *Plasmodium* (spreads via RBC rupture), which have different mechanisms of invasion and dissemination. Understanding the specific apical organelles and their function is crucial for distinguishing *Toxoplasma* pathogenesis.

<a id='question-100'></a>

### Question 100: Medical Parasitology: Part 2 Focus 50 (parasite)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathogenesis of *Toxoplasma gondii* infection, specifically its ability to evade the host immune system by modulating intracellular trafficking and inhibiting apoptosis, leading to the formation of a parasitophorous vacuole (PV) and subsequent chronic infection. (理解弓形虫感染的致病机制，特别是其通过调节细胞内运输和抑制细胞凋亡来逃避宿主免疫系统的能力，从而形成寄生虫囊泡 (PV) 并导致慢性感染。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 35-year-old pregnant woman presents to her primary care physician with complaints of fatigue, low-grade fever, and generalized myalgia for the past 2 weeks. She reports recently adopting a stray cat 3 months ago and has been cleaning its litter box daily. She denies any recent travel or consumption of undercooked meat. Her physical examination is unremarkable except for mild cervical lymphadenopathy. Serological testing reveals positive IgG and IgM antibodies against *Toxoplasma gondii*. A brain biopsy is performed due to concerns about potential congenital transmission and neurological complications, showing multiple ring-enhancing lesions on MRI. Histopathological examination of the brain tissue reveals numerous tachyzoites within glial cells, surrounded by a thin membrane, but without significant inflammatory infiltrate or necrosis. The tachyzoites appear to reside within a modified host cell compartment.

Which of the following mechanisms is MOST likely responsible for the persistence of *Toxoplasma gondii* tachyzoites within the host glial cells, allowing them to evade immune clearance and establish a chronic infection?

(A) Production of a thick polysaccharide capsule that inhibits phagocytosis by microglia and macrophages.
(B) Secretion of a protease that degrades MHC class I molecules on the surface of infected glial cells, preventing recognition by cytotoxic T lymphocytes.
(C) Modulation of intracellular trafficking pathways to form a parasitophorous vacuole (PV) that prevents fusion with lysosomes and inhibits apoptosis.
(D) Induction of massive cytokine release (cytokine storm) that overwhelms the host immune system, leading to immunosuppression.
(E) Direct inhibition of complement activation via the alternative pathway, preventing opsonization and lysis of infected cells.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 35-year-old pregnant woman. Pregnancy increases susceptibility to severe toxoplasmosis and risk of congenital transmission.
- **Symptoms:** Fatigue, low-grade fever, myalgia (common symptoms of acute toxoplasmosis).
- **Exposure History:** Contact with cat litter (primary source of *Toxoplasma gondii* oocysts).
- **Serology:** Positive IgG and IgM indicate acute infection.
- **Imaging:** Brain MRI shows ring-enhancing lesions (characteristic of toxoplasmic encephalitis, especially in immunocompromised individuals, but can occur in immunocompetent individuals, particularly during acute infection or congenital toxoplasmosis).
- **Histopathology:** Tachyzoites within glial cells, surrounded by a thin membrane (suggestive of a modified host cell compartment), minimal inflammation/necrosis (indicates immune evasion).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism of persistence and immune evasion by *Toxoplasma gondii*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- *Toxoplasma gondii* is an obligate intracellular parasite.
- Upon entering a host cell (e.g., glial cell), it secretes proteins from apical organelles (micronemes and rhoptries).
- These secreted proteins modify the host cell membrane at the site of entry, forming a **parasitophorous vacuole (PV)**.
- The PV is a modified host cell compartment derived from the plasma membrane, not an endosome or lysosome.
- Key virulence factors involved in PV formation and maintenance include **MIC proteins (Microneme proteins)** and **GRA proteins (Granule proteins)**.
- The PV membrane actively recruits host cell proteins, including **rab GTPases** (e.g., Rab5, Rab7, Rab11) and **SNARE proteins**, which regulate vesicle trafficking.
- This manipulation of host cell trafficking pathways prevents the PV from fusing with lysosomes, protecting the parasite from degradation.
- Furthermore, *Toxoplasma* actively inhibits apoptosis within the PV, contributing to its persistence.
- This strategy allows the parasite to survive intracellularly, replicate (as tachyzoites in the acute phase), and establish a chronic, latent infection (as bradyzoites within tissue cysts).
- The minimal inflammatory response observed in the histopathology is consistent with the parasite's ability to evade detection and activation of the host immune system within the PV.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) **Production of a thick polysaccharide capsule that inhibits phagocytosis by microglia and macrophages.** While some parasites (e.g., *Cryptococcus neoformans*) use capsules for immune evasion, *Toxoplasma gondii* tachyzoites do not possess a thick polysaccharide capsule. Their primary immune evasion strategy involves intracellular parasitism within the PV.
- (B) **Secretion of a protease that degrades MHC class I molecules on the surface of infected glial cells, preventing recognition by cytotoxic T lymphocytes.** While some viruses (e.g., CMV) downregulate MHC class I, *Toxoplasma* does not primarily rely on MHC class I downregulation for immune evasion. The PV formation is its main mechanism for avoiding immune destruction. Although some studies suggest *Toxoplasma* can modulate MHC expression, it's not the primary mechanism described here.
- (D) **Induction of massive cytokine release (cytokine storm) that overwhelms the host immune system, leading to immunosuppression.** Cytokine storms are characteristic of severe infections with certain viruses (e.g., influenza, SARS-CoV-2) or bacterial sepsis. *Toxoplasma* infection typically does not cause a cytokine storm; instead, it elicits a controlled immune response, although the parasite actively evades this response intracellularly.
- (E) **Direct inhibition of complement activation via the alternative pathway, preventing opsonization and lysis of infected cells.** While complement can play a role in controlling extracellular parasites, *Toxoplasma* is primarily an intracellular parasite. Its main immune evasion strategy occurs *after* entry into the host cell, within the PV, where complement is largely ineffective. The PV prevents access of complement components to the parasite.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | *Toxoplasma gondii*                                  | *Cryptococcus neoformans*                          | Viruses (e.g., CMV)                               | Bacteria (e.g., *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*) |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|---------------------------------------------------|-------------------------------------------------|
| Primary Immune Evasion | Intracellular parasitism within PV; inhibits apoptosis | Polysaccharide capsule; inhibits phagocytosis     | MHC class I downregulation; latency              | Intracellular survival; inhibits phagolysosome fusion |
| Host Cell Compartment | Parasitophorous Vacuole (PV)                          | Extracellular (initially), then phagocytosed      | Infected cell cytoplasm/nucleus                   | Phagosome (often modified)                      |
| Key Virulence Factor | Microneme/Rhoptry proteins (PV formation)             | Capsule                                           | Viral proteins (e.g., IE1 for CMV)                 | Cell wall components (e.g., mycolic acids)       |
| Typical Pathology   | Ring-enhancing lesions, tachyzoites in PVs             | Meningitis, pulmonary infection, encapsulated yeast | CMV inclusions, cell necrosis                    | Granulomas, caseous necrosis                    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
*Toxoplasma gondii* evades the host immune system primarily by residing within a modified host cell compartment called the parasitophorous vacuole (PV), which prevents fusion with lysosomes and inhibits apoptosis. This mechanism allows the parasite to persist intracellularly and establish chronic infection. Differentiate this from capsule-mediated evasion (*Cryptococcus*), MHC downregulation (some viruses), cytokine storm (severe viral/bacterial infections), or complement inhibition (extracellular pathogens).
